People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well, I suppose I came because I’m spending an awful lot of time checking things at home. I keep opening the same folders and drawers, and those little labeled boxes I have—my daughter says I label everything, which is funny because she used to tease me about my handwriting—but I’m looking to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.

Even if I just looked a few minutes earlier, I’ll go back and check again. It takes up more of the day than I’d like.

Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. It’s more that I keep going back to the same places—the folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes. I open them and look to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.

Sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but then I’ll think, “Did I really see it?” and I’m back there again. My daughter says I’ve always been one for labels and little systems—she used to laugh about my neat piles—but now it’s taking far too much of my day.

My daughter says I’ve always been one for labels and little systems—she used to laugh about my neat piles—but now it’s taking far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any other actions in a specific way when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the opening and looking, you know… the same folders, the same drawers, those labeled boxes. But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It takes up more of the day than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you can't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get upset, mostly because if I can’t check, it sits in my mind. It takes up far too much of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.

Sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.

If I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.

But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.

That’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.

It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. I feel I have to go look, just to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… it’s more that they need to be where I left them. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.

It’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.

My husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.

The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.

Though I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.

My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, even when I know better. My husband kept papers for everything, you know, little envelopes and notes… but yes, it’s more that kind of worry than something bad happening to someone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It takes up more of the day than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you can't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get upset, mostly because if I can’t check, it sits in my mind. It takes up far too much of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. It’s more that I keep going back to the same places—the folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes. I open them and look to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but then I’ll think, “Did I really see it?” and I’m back there again. My daughter says I’ve always been one for labels and little systems—she used to laugh about my neat piles—but now it’s taking far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any other actions in a specific way when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the opening and looking, you know… the same folders, the same drawers, those labeled boxes. I’ll lift the lid or pull the folder out and make sure the papers and little keepsakes are still there where I left them.\n\nI don’t think there’s some fancy ritual to it, though my daughter would probably say I’ve always liked things just so—she teases me about all the labels. But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. And then I can’t seem to just let the thought pass. I feel I have to go look, just to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… it’s more that they need to be where I left them. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, even when I know better. My husband kept papers for everything, you know, little envelopes and notes… but yes, it’s more that kind of worry than something bad happening to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake with the items themselves, rather than something bad happening as a result of not checking?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes—that’s right. It’s not that I think the house will burn down or someone will be harmed if I don’t look. It’s more… this little thought keeps needling me, even when I know better, that maybe I’ve thrown away my husband’s old tax papers or put one of the family recipes in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless. My husband used to keep all those papers so neatly—well, neatly to him anyway—and I suppose I get stuck on the idea that I’ve misplaced something important. I can’t seem to let it pass until I check.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. When I open the same folder or drawer and see the papers sitting there, it feels like I can breathe for a moment, like things are where they’re supposed to be.

But then, you know, it doesn’t last very long. I’ll have just checked a few minutes before and still find myself going back to the labeled boxes again, making sure the keepsakes or important papers haven’t somehow been moved. My daughter says I get lost in it—well, she says that about my recipe clippings too, but that’s another matter. It just takes up far too much of my day.

It takes up more of the day than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you can't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get upset, mostly because if I can’t check, it sits in my mind. I keep thinking about those folders and drawers and the little boxes I’ve labeled—my daughter always says I could run a filing office, though I never did anything like that—and I feel like I need to go make sure the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nEven if I only looked a few minutes before, I still want to open them again. It takes up far too much of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. And then I can’t seem to just let the thought pass. I feel I have to go look, just to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… it’s more that they need to be where I left them. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, even when I know better. My husband kept papers for everything, you know, little envelopes and notes… but yes, it’s more that kind of worry than something bad happening to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake with the items themselves, rather than something bad happening as a result of not checking?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes—that’s right. It’s not that I think the house will burn down or someone will be harmed if I don’t look. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not so much symmetrical, no. I’m not lining things up to make them look right, if that’s what you mean.

It’s more that I keep opening the same folders, drawers, those labeled boxes—I have labels on nearly everything now, my daughter helped me with some of them—and I check that the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I was just there a few minutes before. Then I think, “Well, did I really see it?” and back I go again. It eats up far too much of the day.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I came because I’m spending an awful lot of time checking things at home. I keep opening the same folders and drawers, and those little labeled boxes I have—my daughter says I label everything, which is funny because she used to tease me about my handwriting—but I’m looking to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.\n\nEven if I just looked a few minutes earlier, I’ll go back and check again. It takes up more of the day than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you can't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get upset, mostly because if I can’t check, it sits in my mind. I keep thinking about those folders and drawers and the little boxes I’ve labeled—my daughter always says I could run a filing office, though I never did anything like that—and I feel like I need to go make sure the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nEven if I only looked a few minutes before, I still want to open them again. It takes up far too much of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. It’s more that I keep going back to the same places—the folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes. I open them and look to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but then I’ll think, “Did I really see it?” and I’m back there again. My daughter says I’ve always been one for labels and little systems—she used to laugh about my neat piles—but now it’s taking far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any other actions in a specific way when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the opening and looking, you know… the same folders, the same drawers, those labeled boxes. I’ll lift the lid or pull the folder out and make sure the papers and little keepsakes are still there where I left them.\n\nI don’t think there’s some fancy ritual to it, though my daughter would probably say I’ve always liked things just so—she teases me about all the labels. But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. And then I can’t seem to just let the thought pass. I feel I have to go look, just to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… it’s more that they need to be where I left them. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, even when I know better. My husband kept papers for everything, you know, little envelopes and notes… but yes, it’s more that kind of worry than something bad happening to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake with the items themselves, rather than something bad happening as a result of not checking?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes—that’s right. It’s not that I think the house will burn down or someone will be harmed if I don’t look. My husband used to keep all those papers so neatly—well, neatly to him anyway—and I suppose I get stuck on the idea that I’ve misplaced something important. I can’t seem to let it pass until I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps you regain a sense of control over your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. When I open the same folder or drawer and see the papers sitting there, it feels like I can breathe for a moment, like things are where they’re supposed to be.\n\nBut then, you know, it doesn’t last very long. I’ll have just checked a few minutes before and still find myself going back to the labeled boxes again, making sure the keepsakes or important papers haven’t somehow been moved. My daughter says I get lost in it—well, she says that about my recipe clippings too, but that’s another matter. It just takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you make sure things are symmetrical or exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Not so much symmetrical, no. I’m not lining things up to make them look right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I keep opening the same folders, drawers, those labeled boxes—I have labels on nearly everything now, my daughter helped me with some of them—and I check that the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I was just there a few minutes before. Then I think, “Well, did I really see it?” and back I go again. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I came because I’m spending an awful lot of time checking things at home. I keep opening the same folders and drawers, and those little labeled boxes I have—my daughter says I label everything, which is funny because she used to tease me about my handwriting—but I’m looking to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.\n\nEven if I just looked a few minutes earlier, I’ll go back and check again. It takes up more of the day than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you can't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get upset, mostly because if I can’t check, it sits in my mind. I keep thinking about those folders and drawers and the little boxes I’ve labeled—my daughter always says I could run a filing office, though I never did anything like that—and I feel like I need to go make sure the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nEven if I only looked a few minutes before, I still want to open them again. It takes up far too much of the day, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check these things in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Well… not always in a strict order, I don’t think. It’s more that I keep going back to the same places—the folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes. I open them and look to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but then I’ll think, “Did I really see it?” and I’m back there again. My daughter says I’ve always been one for labels and little systems—she used to laugh about my neat piles—but now it’s taking far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform any other actions in a specific way when you're checking these things?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the opening and looking, you know… the same folders, the same drawers, those labeled boxes. I’ll lift the lid or pull the folder out and make sure the papers and little keepsakes are still there where I left them.\n\nI don’t think there’s some fancy ritual to it, though my daughter would probably say I’ve always liked things just so—she teases me about all the labels. But really I just keep going back and doing it again, even if I was there only a few minutes before. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried something bad will happen if you don't check these things?\n\nPatient: Yes… not like the house will burn down or anything like that. It’s more this nagging thought that I’ve done something terribly careless.\n\nEven when I know better, I’ll think, “What if I threw away my husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if I put that family recipe in the wrong box?” My mother had recipe cards, you know, written in that tiny neat handwriting people used to have—my daughter says nobody writes like that anymore. And then I can’t seem to just let the thought pass. I feel I have to go look, just to be sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a specific order or arrangement for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… it’s more that they need to be where I left them. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. The folders in the drawer, the keepsakes in the labeled boxes, that sort of thing.\n\nI’ll open the same places over and over—sometimes I’ve only just looked—and I still feel I have to make sure the papers are there. My daughter helped me with some of the labels years ago, actually, because my handwriting isn’t what it used to be… but anyway, yes, it’s the checking that takes up so much of the day, not arranging them into some perfect order.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is mostly to prevent yourself from making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… mostly to make sure I haven’t made some awful mistake with the papers or put something important where it doesn’t belong.\n\nI’ll open the same folders, the drawers, those labeled boxes—sometimes I only checked them a few minutes earlier—and I still feel pulled back to look again. It’s embarrassing how much of the day it takes. My daughter says the labels are very “me,” you know, because I’ve always kept things neat, but it’s not really about neatness. It’s that I need to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried that the things you're checking might be contaminated or unclean in some way?\n\nPatient: Contaminated… unclean? No, I don’t think that’s really it. I mean, I like things tidy, of course—my mother was very particular about clean linens, she had a whole way of folding them that I never quite got right—but with the folders and boxes, I’m not worried they’re dirty.\n\nIt’s more… well, I suppose I’m just trying to make sure they’re still there. The cleanliness part isn’t what pulls me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid something bad might happen if you don't, rather than just being afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I picture some terrible thing happening, exactly… it’s more that I can’t settle unless I’ve opened the folder or the drawer or one of those labeled boxes and seen that the important papers and the keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nSometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, and I tell myself, “You already looked,” but then I’m back at it again. My daughter always says I’ve kept papers better than anyone she knows—though half the time I think she’s just being kind—but it’s taken over too much of the day now. So… yes, it’s not so much a disaster in my mind, more needing to make sure they’re there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be in a perfectly accurate or exact state for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Perfectly accurate… I suppose, in a way, yes. Not like the corners have to line up exactly—though my daughter teases me about my labels, says I should’ve been an archivist—but it’s more that the papers and keepsakes have to be where I believe I left them.\n\nIf I don’t open the folder or the drawer and actually see them there, I can’t quite feel easy. Even if I checked just a few minutes ago, I’ll think, “Well, maybe I should make sure again,” and then there I am, opening the same boxes. It takes up far too much of the day, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things more about preventing a mistake or about preventing something bad from happening if you don't check?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about preventing a mistake, if that’s the choice. Not a mistake like writing the wrong number on a check—though I did that once years ago and the bank clerk was very patient with me—but more that I worry I’ve misplaced something important.\n\nThat’s why I keep opening the same folders and drawers and those labeled boxes, just to see the papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I only looked a few minutes before, I feel pulled back to check again. It’s silly, I know, but it eats up so much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven more by the thought of something terrible happening if you don't, or by the fear of having made an error?\n\nPatient: It’s more the fear that I’ve made some awful mistake, I think. I don’t usually imagine a fire or someone being hurt or anything like that.\n\nIt’s that little thought that keeps poking at me—“What if you threw away your husband’s old tax papers?” or “What if that family recipe got put in the wrong box?” And then I feel like I’ve been terribly careless somehow, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t do anything of the sort.\n\nMy husband was very particular about his papers, you know—he had a way of folding receipts that I never understood—but, yes, I suppose the checking is because I can’t let that thought pass until I look again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking itself has to be done in a certain way to feel complete?\n\nPatient: Not in a special order, no… I don’t think I have a little ceremony for it, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that I have to actually open the folder, or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes and see the papers or the keepsakes sitting there.\n\nThough I do tend to look a bit longer than I need to—my daughter says I “take inventory,” which is what my mother used to do with the pantry before holidays. But really, it’s the same checking over and over. I’ll have just looked a few minutes before, and then I feel I ought to check again, just to be sure they’re where I left them. It takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things driven by a fear that something bad will happen to someone or something you care about if you don't check?\n\nPatient: No, not exactly someone getting hurt or anything like that. It’s more that I get this thought needling at me—like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I can’t seem to just let it pass. I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless, even when I know better. My husband kept papers for everything, you know, little envelopes and notes… but yes, it’s more that kind of worry than something bad happening to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check these things mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake with the items themselves, rather than something bad happening as a result of not checking?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes—that’s right. It’s not that I think the house will burn down or someone will be harmed if I don’t look. It’s more… this little thought keeps needling me, even when I know better, that maybe I’ve thrown away my husband’s old tax papers or put one of the family recipes in the wrong box.\n\nAnd then I feel as if I’ve done something terribly careless. My husband used to keep all those papers so neatly—well, neatly to him anyway—and I suppose I get stuck on the idea that I’ve misplaced something important. I can’t seem to let it pass until I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things helps you regain a sense of control over your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. When I open the same folder or drawer and see the papers sitting there, it feels like I can breathe for a moment, like things are where they’re supposed to be.\n\nBut then, you know, it doesn’t last very long. I’ll have just checked a few minutes before and still find myself going back to the labeled boxes again, making sure the keepsakes or important papers haven’t somehow been moved. My daughter says I get lost in it—well, she says that about my recipe clippings too, but that’s another matter. It just takes up far too much of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking helps you make sure things are symmetrical or exactly as they should be?\n\nPatient: Not so much symmetrical, no. I’m not lining things up to make them look right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I keep opening the same folders, drawers, those labeled boxes—I have labels on nearly everything now, my daughter helped me with some of them—and I check that the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Even if I was just there a few minutes before. Then I think, “Well, did I really see it?” and back I go again. It eats up far too much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you're checking need to be exactly as you left them, down to the smallest detail?\n\nPatient: Yes… well, not every tiny detail, like if a corner of a paper is angled differently. I don’t think that’s quite it.\n\nIt’s more that I have to know the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. I’ll open the same folder, or a drawer, or one of those labeled boxes—my daughter says I have labels on labels now—and even if I just looked a few minutes ago, I’ll feel pulled to check again. Then I lose track of time, and suddenly half the morning’s gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My daughter says I get lost in it—well, she says that about my recipe clippings too, but that’s another matter.
